========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hi doc. Thanks for seeing me. I, uh... I guess I'm here because I just can't seem to shake this feeling lately. I came in because I just feel so worn out, like, almost every single day. 

And it's not just a normal tired, you know? Like, I work as a cashier down at the grocery store—have been for a few years now—and lately, I just... I get up for my shift, and even if I've been in bed for a full eight hours, maybe even nine, my body just feels like it weighs a thousand pounds. My head is dragging, my legs are heavy, and I'm just standing there at the register scanning items and bagging groceries, and it takes everything in me just to keep going. 

I actually thought maybe it was my mattress, so I tried flipping it last weekend, and then I thought maybe I was drinking too much soda in the afternoons, so I cut back on that, but no matter what I do, I just wake up feeling completely drained before the day even starts. It's just... I don't understand why a full night's sleep doesn't fix it anymore, and it makes getting through a whole shift just completely exhausting.

Hmm, no, I wouldn't really say that. I mean, I still like doing the things I've always liked. 

Like, take bowling for example. I go with this group of guys from high school—me, my buddy Dave, and sometimes my cousin Mike—we go down to the lanes on Route 9 every other Thursday. I still really look forward to that. Even when I'm dragging my feet and feeling like a total zombie from standing at the grocery register all day, once I get there and get my shoes on, I'm having a great time. I still laugh at Dave's terrible jokes, you know? And on the weekends, I still love firing up the PlayStation. I've been playing this one game for like... man, maybe three months now? I still get really into the story and all that. It's not like I sit there and feel bored by it or anything. 

If anything, I just wish I had more energy to do those things, you know? Because the fun is definitely still there. I still want to go to the movies, I still like grilling out when the weather's nice... like last week I made these jalapeño burgers, spent a good hour prepping the meat and getting the charcoal just right, and I really enjoyed it. So, yeah, the enjoyment hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just my battery is completely drained. But the actual liking of things? No, that's all still there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, that's... that's a big part of it, actually. It's the falling asleep part that really gets me. Like I was saying before about being so tired, you'd think the second my head hits the pillow, I'd just be out cold, right? But it's almost like the opposite happens. 

I'll get into bed, usually around 10:30 or 11, and I'll be absolutely exhausted from standing all day, but then my brain just... turns on. It's like someone flipped a switch. I just lie there, staring at the ceiling, and my mind starts racing. I start going over the electric bill that's due next week, wondering if I picked up enough extra shifts to cover it, and then I start thinking about my schedule at the store—like, if my manager is going to put me on the closing shift again on Friday, which always messes up my weekend. And then I start worrying about my mom, you know, she lives a couple towns over and her car has been making this weird noise, and I'm lying there wondering if she's okay, if she's going to break down on the highway. 

It just goes on and on like that for hours. And then, even when I finally do manage to drift off—maybe around 2 or 3 in the morning—it's not a good sleep. I toss and turn, I get tangled in the sheets. When my alarm goes off at 7, I wake up feeling like my brain never actually shut down. It's like I was just resting my eyes but my mind was still running a marathon all night. So yeah, I definitely wake up feeling completely unrefreshed, like I never really settled down at all.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hmm, it's... I don't think it's a lack of motivation, really. Like I said before, I still want to do things. I want to go bowling, I want to grill those burgers, I want to play my games. The drive to actually do stuff is there. 

But the energy thing... it's tricky. Because, honestly, I'm almost always physically tired. Like, I'm on my feet for eight hours a day at the store, standing on those hard linoleum floors, reaching across the scanner, bagging heavy stuff like dog food and gallons of milk. So my body is always feeling it. I'm not really sure I have moments where I'm *not* physically tired anymore, you know? 

Even on my days off, like Sundays, when I don't have to go into the store and I can just sit on the couch in my sweatpants... I still feel completely drained. But I think that goes back to the sleep thing I was just talking about. Because my brain is constantly going, worrying about the bills, my schedule, my mom's car, whatever else pops into my head at 1 AM... I never really get a chance to recharge. So even when my body has been resting on the couch all day, I still feel this heavy, dragging exhaustion. It's not that I don't want to get up and do something, it's just that my battery is always at zero, even when I haven't done anything physical that day. So, yeah, I guess I'm not really sure how to separate the physical tiredness from everything else, because it just feels like it's all rolled into one big ball of exhaustion all the time.

I start going over the electric bill that's due next week, wondering if I picked up enough extra shifts to cover it, and then I start thinking about my schedule at the store—like, if my manager is going to put me on the closing shift again on Friday, which always messes up my weekend. And then I start worrying about my mom, you know, she lives a couple towns over and her car has been making this weird noise, and I'm lying there wondering if she's okay, if she's going to break down on the highway. \n\nIt just goes on and on like that for hours. And then, even when I finally do manage to drift off—maybe around 2 or 3 in the morning—it's not a good sleep. I toss and turn, I get tangled in the sheets. When my alarm goes off at 7, I wake up feeling like my brain never actually shut down. It's like I was just resting my eyes but my mind was still running a marathon all night. So yeah, I definitely wake up feeling completely unrefreshed, like I never really settled down at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your energy level or a lack of motivation, even when you're not physically tired?\n\nPatient: Well, hmm, it's... I don't think it's a lack of motivation, really. Like I said before, I still want to do things. I want to go bowling, I want to grill those burgers, I want to play my games. The drive to actually do stuff is there. \n\nBut the energy thing... it's tricky. Because, honestly, I'm almost always physically tired. Like, I'm on my feet for eight hours a day at the store, standing on those hard linoleum floors, reaching across the scanner, bagging heavy stuff like dog food and gallons of milk. So my body is always feeling it. I'm not really sure I have moments where I'm *not* physically tired anymore, you know? \n\nEven on my days off, like Sundays, when I don't have to go into the store and I can just sit on the couch in my sweatpants... I still feel completely drained. But I think that goes back to the sleep thing I was just talking about. Because my brain is constantly going, worrying about the bills, my schedule, my mom's car, whatever else pops into my head at 1 AM... I never really get a chance to recharge. So even when my body has been resting on the couch all day, I still feel this heavy, dragging exhaustion. It's not that I don't want to get up and do something, it's just that my battery is always at zero, even when I haven't done anything physical that day. So, yeah, I guess I'm not really sure how to separate the physical tiredness from everything else, because it just feels like it's all rolled into one big ball of exhaustion all the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or hopeless. It's not like that. I mean, sure, I get stressed out, like I was saying about the bills and my mom's car and work and all that. But it's not a sad feeling, you know? It's more of a... a buzzing feeling. Like my brain is just constantly humming with all these things I need to figure out or keep track of. 

I don't wake up in the morning feeling like there's a dark cloud over me or like there's no point in getting out of bed. I mean, I *do* have trouble getting out of bed, but that's just because I'm so exhausted from not sleeping, like I told you. It's not because I feel hopeless about the day. I actually look forward to things, like my days off, or seeing Dave and Mike at the bowling alley. 

I know some people get really down in the dumps, like my buddy Dave's brother went through a really rough patch a few years ago where he wouldn't even leave the house for weeks, just sat in the dark watching reruns. But I'm not like that at all. I go to work, I talk to the customers—even the ones who complain about the coupon policies, which, let me tell you, happens at least three times a shift—and I still try to make the best of things. I just feel wound up all the time, not down. So, no, I definitely wouldn't use words like depressed or hopeless to describe how I'm feeling. Just... overwhelmed, I guess. But definitely not sad.

So, yeah, I guess I'm not really sure how to separate the physical tiredness from everything else, because it just feels like it's all rolled into one big ball of exhaustion all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or hopeless more often than usual, even if just for a little while?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or hopeless. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. That's exactly it. It's... it's just really hard to control. I mean, I've always been a guy who thinks ahead, but lately, it just feels like my mind is always jumping from one thing to the next, and I can't hit the brakes at all. 

Like, I'll just be standing there at the register, right? Scanning a box of cereal, and suddenly I'm stressing about money—which is always tight, but now it's just eating at me constantly. And then, before I even finish bagging the groceries, my brain jumps to my house, because we have this relative staying with us right now. It was supposed to be a short-term thing, but it's dragging out, and my wife is just so incredibly stressed about it. The house feels crowded, she's on edge all the time, and that makes *me* stressed because I hate seeing her upset and I'm constantly trying to keep the peace at home. 

But it doesn't stop there. Then, boom, my brain is off to something else, like my church duties. I help out on Sundays, setting things up, and I'm constantly terrified I'm going to forget something important or mess up the schedule and let everyone down. And then, while I'm worrying about church, I'm suddenly right back to worrying about whether I'm messing up at work—like, did I give that last lady the right change? Is my manager watching me on the cameras thinking I'm not scanning fast enough? Am I going to get written up? 

It's just this endless loop. Bouncing from the money, to the relative in our spare room, to my wife being overwhelmed, to church, to work. I just can't seem to shut it off or focus on just one thing, because the second I try to calm down about one worry, three more just pop up to take its place. It's like I'm playing whack-a-mole in my head all day long.

So, yeah, the enjoyment hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just my battery is completely drained. But the actual liking of things? No, that's all still there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or do you wake up feeling unrefreshed even after a full night's sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, that's... that's a big part of it, actually. It's the falling asleep part that really gets me. Like I was saying before about being so tired, you'd think the second my head hits the pillow, I'd just be out cold, right? But it's almost like the opposite happens. \n\nI'll get into bed, usually around 10:30 or 11, and I'll be absolutely exhausted from standing all day, but then my brain just... turns on. It's like someone flipped a switch. I just lie there, staring at the ceiling, and my mind starts racing. I start going over the electric bill that's due next week, wondering if I picked up enough extra shifts to cover it, and then I start thinking about my schedule at the store—like, if my manager is going to put me on the closing shift again on Friday, which always messes up my weekend. And then I start worrying about my mom, you know, she lives a couple towns over and her car has been making this weird noise, and I'm lying there wondering if she's okay, if she's going to break down on the highway. \n\nIt just goes on and on like that for hours. And then, even when I finally do manage to drift off—maybe around 2 or 3 in the morning—it's not a good sleep. I toss and turn, I get tangled in the sheets. When my alarm goes off at 7, I wake up feeling like my brain never actually shut down. It's like I was just resting my eyes but my mind was still running a marathon all night. So yeah, I definitely wake up feeling completely unrefreshed, like I never really settled down at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your energy level or a lack of motivation, even when you're not physically tired?\n\nPatient: Well, hmm, it's... I don't think it's a lack of motivation, really. Like I said before, I still want to do things. I want to go bowling, I want to grill those burgers, I want to play my games. The drive to actually do stuff is there. \n\nBut the energy thing... it's tricky. Because, honestly, I'm almost always physically tired. Like, I'm on my feet for eight hours a day at the store, standing on those hard linoleum floors, reaching across the scanner, bagging heavy stuff like dog food and gallons of milk. So my body is always feeling it. I'm not really sure I have moments where I'm *not* physically tired anymore, you know? \n\nEven on my days off, like Sundays, when I don't have to go into the store and I can just sit on the couch in my sweatpants... I still feel completely drained. But I think that goes back to the sleep thing I was just talking about. Because my brain is constantly going, worrying about the bills, my schedule, my mom's car, whatever else pops into my head at 1 AM... I never really get a chance to recharge. So even when my body has been resting on the couch all day, I still feel this heavy, dragging exhaustion. It's not that I don't want to get up and do something, it's just that my battery is always at zero, even when I haven't done anything physical that day. So, yeah, I guess I'm not really sure how to separate the physical tiredness from everything else, because it just feels like it's all rolled into one big ball of exhaustion all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or hopeless more often than usual, even if just for a little while?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or hopeless. It's not like that. I mean, sure, I get stressed out, like I was saying about the bills and my mom's car and work and all that. But it's not a sad feeling, you know? It's more of a... a buzzing feeling. Like my brain is just constantly humming with all these things I need to figure out or keep track of. \n\nI don't wake up in the morning feeling like there's a dark cloud over me or like there's no point in getting out of bed. I mean, I *do* have trouble getting out of bed, but that's just because I'm so exhausted from not sleeping, like I told you. It's not because I feel hopeless about the day. I actually look forward to things, like my days off, or seeing Dave and Mike at the bowling alley. \n\nI know some people get really down in the dumps, like my buddy Dave's brother went through a really rough patch a few years ago where he wouldn't even leave the house for weeks, just sat in the dark watching reruns. But I'm not like that at all. I go to work, I talk to the customers—even the ones who complain about the coupon policies, which, let me tell you, happens at least three times a shift—and I still try to make the best of things. I just feel wound up all the time, not down. So, no, I definitely wouldn't use words like depressed or hopeless to describe how I'm feeling. Just... overwhelmed, I guess. But definitely not sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry a lot more than you used to, or that your worries are hard to control, especially about things like work, money, or family?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That's exactly it. It's... it's just really hard to control. I mean, I've always been a guy who thinks ahead, but lately, it just feels like my mind is always jumping from one thing to the next, and I can't hit the brakes at all. \n\nLike, I'll just be standing there at the register, right? Scanning a box of cereal, and suddenly I'm stressing about money—which is always tight, but now it's just eating at me constantly. And then, before I even finish bagging the groceries, my brain jumps to my house, because we have this relative staying with us right now. It was supposed to be a short-term thing, but it's dragging out, and my wife is just so incredibly stressed about it. The house feels crowded, she's on edge all the time, and that makes *me* stressed because I hate seeing her upset and I'm constantly trying to keep the peace at home. \n\nBut it doesn't stop there. Then, boom, my brain is off to something else, like my church duties. I help out on Sundays, setting things up, and I'm constantly terrified I'm going to forget something important or mess up the schedule and let everyone down. And then, while I'm worrying about church, I'm suddenly right back to worrying about whether I'm messing up at work—like, did I give that last lady the right change? Is my manager watching me on the cameras thinking I'm not scanning fast enough? Am I going to get written up? \n\nIt's just this endless loop. Bouncing from the money, to the relative in our spare room, to my wife being overwhelmed, to church, to work. I just can't seem to shut it off or focus on just one thing, because the second I try to calm down about one worry, three more just pop up to take its place. It's like I'm playing whack-a-mole in my head all day long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I mean, I *do* have trouble getting out of bed, but that's just because I'm so exhausted from not sleeping, like I told you. It's not because I feel hopeless about the day. I actually look forward to things, like my days off, or seeing Dave and Mike at the bowling alley. \n\nI know some people get really down in the dumps, like my buddy Dave's brother went through a really rough patch a few years ago where he wouldn't even leave the house for weeks, just sat in the dark watching reruns. But I'm not like that at all. I go to work, I talk to the customers—even the ones who complain about the coupon policies, which, let me tell you, happens at least three times a shift—and I still try to make the best of things. I just feel wound up all the time, not down. So, no, I definitely wouldn't use words like depressed or hopeless to describe how I'm feeling. Just... overwhelmed, I guess. But definitely not sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry a lot more than you used to, or that your worries are hard to control, especially about things like work, money, or family?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That's exactly it. It's... it's just really hard to control. I mean, I've always been a guy who thinks ahead, but lately, it just feels like my mind is always jumping from one thing to the next, and I can't hit the brakes at all. \n\nLike, I'll just be standing there at the register, right? Scanning a box of cereal, and suddenly I'm stressing about money—which is always tight, but now it's just eating at me constantly. And then, before I even finish bagging the groceries, my brain jumps to my house, because we have this relative staying with us right now. It was supposed to be a short-term thing, but it's dragging out, and my wife is just so incredibly stressed about it. The house feels crowded, she's on edge all the time, and that makes *me* stressed because I hate seeing her upset and I'm constantly trying to keep the peace at home. \n\nBut it doesn't stop there. Then, boom, my brain is off to something else, like my church duties. I help out on Sundays, setting things up, and I'm constantly terrified I'm going to forget something important or mess up the schedule and let everyone down. And then, while I'm worrying about church, I'm suddenly right back to worrying about whether I'm messing up at work—like, did I give that last lady the right change? Is my manager watching me on the cameras thinking I'm not scanning fast enough? Am I going to get written up? \n\nIt's just this endless loop. Bouncing from the money, to the relative in our spare room, to my wife being overwhelmed, to church, to work. I just can't seem to shut it off or focus on just one thing, because the second I try to calm down about one worry, three more just pop up to take its place. It's like I'm playing whack-a-mole in my head all day long.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that these worries happen almost every day, and do they feel difficult to control once they start?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / cashier / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Uncontrollable_Worry\nmanifestation: \"I try telling myself to pray on it or just leave it alone, but once a worry starts it keeps rolling into another one until I'm thinking through every bad way the day could go.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it's... it's pretty much every single day. I can't really think of a day lately where I haven't been dealing with it. And as for controlling it, well, that's the really frustrating part, you know? Because I try. I really do try to stop it. 

Like, I'll be standing there, feeling myself getting all worked up about the bills or the situation at the house with my wife and that relative, and I'll tell myself, 'Just stop. Pray on it. Leave it in God's hands, or just leave it alone for now.' I'll literally try to force myself to just focus on the customer in front of me or the bagging. But... it doesn't work. It's like, once a worry starts, it just gains momentum. It's like a snowball rolling down a hill, you know? It starts small, just one little thought about money, and then it picks up speed and rolls right into worrying about the house, and then it rolls into worrying about my job, and before I know it, I'm just standing there thinking through every single bad way the entire day could go. 

I'll be imagining getting fired, then coming home to my wife crying because the house is a mess and she's stressed, and then not being able to pay the electric bill... it just spirals. And I'm just standing there holding a can of soup, trying to breathe, but my mind is already ten steps ahead into total disaster mode. So, yeah, I try to shut it down, I try to pray on it, but once it gets going, it just feels impossible to actually stop the train, if that makes sense. It just keeps rolling and rolling until I'm totally exhausted by my own thoughts.

So even when my body has been resting on the couch all day, I still feel this heavy, dragging exhaustion. It's not that I don't want to get up and do something, it's just that my battery is always at zero, even when I haven't done anything physical that day. So, yeah, I guess I'm not really sure how to separate the physical tiredness from everything else, because it just feels like it's all rolled into one big ball of exhaustion all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or hopeless more often than usual, even if just for a little while?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or hopeless. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really physically. I mean, my mind is going a million miles an hour, like I was just saying, but my body... no, I'm not pacing around or bouncing my leg or anything like that. Actually, it's kind of the opposite, to be honest. Because I'm on my feet all day at the grocery store—they give us those little black rubber anti-fatigue mats to stand on behind the register, but let me tell you, after an eight-hour shift of standing in one square foot of space, they don't do a single thing for your lower back or your heels. My feet are always killing me. 

By the time I clock out and get to my car, I'm just totally wiped out. So when I get home, even with the house being as chaotic as it is right now with our relative staying there and my wife being all on edge, I just want to sink into the couch. I don't have this urge to get up and pace the hallways or anything. I mean, sometimes I'll just sit there in my recliner—it's this old brown one we got on clearance a few years ago, really comfortable—and I'll just sit there and stare at the TV. I might not even be paying attention to the show, like I could be watching a whole baseball game and not know the score, because my brain is doing that whole whack-a-mole thing with my worries. But physically? No, I'm pretty still. I'm honestly just too exhausted to be restless. So I wouldn't say I feel wired in my body, you know, just... just in my head.

Because I'm on my feet all day at the grocery store—they give us those little black rubber anti-fatigue mats to stand on behind the register, but let me tell you, after an eight-hour shift of standing in one square foot of space, they don't do a single thing for your lower back or your heels. My feet are always killing me. \n\nBy the time I clock out and get to my car, I'm just totally wiped out. So when I get home, even with the house being as chaotic as it is right now with our relative staying there and my wife being all on edge, I just want to sink into the couch. I don't have this urge to get up and pace the hallways or anything. I mean, sometimes I'll just sit there in my recliner—it's this old brown one we got on clearance a few years ago, really comfortable—and I'll just sit there and stare at the TV. I might not even be paying attention to the show, like I could be watching a whole baseball game and not know the score, because my brain is doing that whole whack-a-mole thing with my worries. But physically? No, I'm pretty still. I'm honestly just too exhausted to be restless. So I wouldn't say I feel wired in my body, you know, just... just in my head.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever find yourself dozing off or falling asleep during the day, even when you're trying to stay awake, like while sitting quietly or watching TV?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / cashier / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, actually, that's the funny thing about it. You'd think I would, right? Like I was just saying, I get home and I am just physically beat from standing on that thin little mat all day, and I sink right into that old recliner. My wife even tells me sometimes, she'll look over at me and say, "Why don't you just close your eyes and take a nap?" because she can see how totally drained I look. And we'll have the TV on—usually it's a baseball game, or maybe one of those home renovation shows she likes where they're always knocking down kitchen walls, just something playing in the background. And I'll be sitting there, and I might even *want* to sleep, just to get a break from everything going on in my head. But doze off? No. I never fall asleep. 

It's like my body is made of lead, just totally heavy and done for the day, but my brain won't give me the green light. Because even though I'm sitting perfectly still and trying to relax, my mind is still back at the grocery store, or it's stressing about our relative in the spare room, or calculating the electric bill. I'll just be staring at the screen, completely wide awake, hearing the TV but not really processing it because my thoughts are just too loud. So, no, I never accidentally fall asleep or doze off during the day, even when I'm just sitting quietly. I honestly wish I could. A nap sounds amazing in theory, you know? But my mind just absolutely refuses to shut down enough to let me actually cross over into sleeping. I just sit there awake, feeling exhausted.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, G47.00, G47.01\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi doc. Thanks for seeing me. I, uh... I guess I'm here because I just can't seem to shake this feeling lately. I came in because I just feel so worn out, like, almost every single day. \n\nAnd it's not just a normal tired, you know? Like, I work as a cashier down at the grocery store—have been for a few years now—and lately, I just... I get up for my shift, and even if I've been in bed for a full eight hours, maybe even nine, my body just feels like it weighs a thousand pounds. My head is dragging, my legs are heavy, and I'm just standing there at the register scanning items and bagging groceries, and it takes everything in me just to keep going. \n\nI actually thought maybe it was my mattress, so I tried flipping it last weekend, and then I thought maybe I was drinking too much soda in the afternoons, so I cut back on that, but no matter what I do, I just wake up feeling completely drained before the day even starts. It's just... I don't understand why a full night's sleep doesn't fix it anymore, and it makes getting through a whole shift just completely exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you once found pleasurable or interesting?\n\nPatient: Hmm, no, I wouldn't really say that. I mean, I still like doing the things I've always liked. \n\nLike, take bowling for example. I go with this group of guys from high school—me, my buddy Dave, and sometimes my cousin Mike—we go down to the lanes on Route 9 every other Thursday. I still really look forward to that. Even when I'm dragging my feet and feeling like a total zombie from standing at the grocery register all day, once I get there and get my shoes on, I'm having a great time. I still laugh at Dave's terrible jokes, you know? And on the weekends, I still love firing up the PlayStation. I've been playing this one game for like... man, maybe three months now? I still get really into the story and all that. It's not like I sit there and feel bored by it or anything. \n\nIf anything, I just wish I had more energy to do those things, you know? Because the fun is definitely still there. I still want to go to the movies, I still like grilling out when the weather's nice... like last week I made these jalapeño burgers, spent a good hour prepping the meat and getting the charcoal just right, and I really enjoyed it. So, yeah, the enjoyment hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just my battery is completely drained. But the actual liking of things? No, that's all still there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or do you wake up feeling unrefreshed even after a full night's sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, that's... that's a big part of it, actually. It's the falling asleep part that really gets me. Like I was saying before about being so tired, you'd think the second my head hits the pillow, I'd just be out cold, right? But it's almost like the opposite happens. \n\nI'll get into bed, usually around 10:30 or 11, and I'll be absolutely exhausted from standing all day, but then my brain just... turns on. It's like someone flipped a switch. I just lie there, staring at the ceiling, and my mind starts racing. I start going over the electric bill that's due next week, wondering if I picked up enough extra shifts to cover it, and then I start thinking about my schedule at the store—like, if my manager is going to put me on the closing shift again on Friday, which always messes up my weekend. And then I start worrying about my mom, you know, she lives a couple towns over and her car has been making this weird noise, and I'm lying there wondering if she's okay, if she's going to break down on the highway. \n\nIt just goes on and on like that for hours. And then, even when I finally do manage to drift off—maybe around 2 or 3 in the morning—it's not a good sleep. I toss and turn, I get tangled in the sheets. When my alarm goes off at 7, I wake up feeling like my brain never actually shut down. It's like I was just resting my eyes but my mind was still running a marathon all night. So yeah, I definitely wake up feeling completely unrefreshed, like I never really settled down at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your energy level or a lack of motivation, even when you're not physically tired?\n\nPatient: Well, hmm, it's... I don't think it's a lack of motivation, really. Like I said before, I still want to do things. I want to go bowling, I want to grill those burgers, I want to play my games. The drive to actually do stuff is there. \n\nBut the energy thing... it's tricky. Because, honestly, I'm almost always physically tired. Like, I'm on my feet for eight hours a day at the store, standing on those hard linoleum floors, reaching across the scanner, bagging heavy stuff like dog food and gallons of milk. So my body is always feeling it. I'm not really sure I have moments where I'm *not* physically tired anymore, you know? \n\nEven on my days off, like Sundays, when I don't have to go into the store and I can just sit on the couch in my sweatpants... I still feel completely drained. But I think that goes back to the sleep thing I was just talking about. Because my brain is constantly going, worrying about the bills, my schedule, my mom's car, whatever else pops into my head at 1 AM... I never really get a chance to recharge. So even when my body has been resting on the couch all day, I still feel this heavy, dragging exhaustion. It's not that I don't want to get up and do something, it's just that my battery is always at zero, even when I haven't done anything physical that day. So, yeah, I guess I'm not really sure how to separate the physical tiredness from everything else, because it just feels like it's all rolled into one big ball of exhaustion all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or hopeless more often than usual, even if just for a little while?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or hopeless. It's not like that. I mean, sure, I get stressed out, like I was saying about the bills and my mom's car and work and all that. But it's not a sad feeling, you know? It's more of a... a buzzing feeling. Like my brain is just constantly humming with all these things I need to figure out or keep track of. \n\nI don't wake up in the morning feeling like there's a dark cloud over me or like there's no point in getting out of bed. I mean, I *do* have trouble getting out of bed, but that's just because I'm so exhausted from not sleeping, like I told you. It's not because I feel hopeless about the day. I actually look forward to things, like my days off, or seeing Dave and Mike at the bowling alley. \n\nI know some people get really down in the dumps, like my buddy Dave's brother went through a really rough patch a few years ago where he wouldn't even leave the house for weeks, just sat in the dark watching reruns. But I'm not like that at all. I go to work, I talk to the customers—even the ones who complain about the coupon policies, which, let me tell you, happens at least three times a shift—and I still try to make the best of things. I just feel wound up all the time, not down. So, no, I definitely wouldn't use words like depressed or hopeless to describe how I'm feeling. Just... overwhelmed, I guess. But definitely not sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry a lot more than you used to, or that your worries are hard to control, especially about things like work, money, or family?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That's exactly it. It's... it's just really hard to control. I mean, I've always been a guy who thinks ahead, but lately, it just feels like my mind is always jumping from one thing to the next, and I can't hit the brakes at all. \n\nLike, I'll just be standing there at the register, right? Scanning a box of cereal, and suddenly I'm stressing about money—which is always tight, but now it's just eating at me constantly. And then, before I even finish bagging the groceries, my brain jumps to my house, because we have this relative staying with us right now. It was supposed to be a short-term thing, but it's dragging out, and my wife is just so incredibly stressed about it. The house feels crowded, she's on edge all the time, and that makes *me* stressed because I hate seeing her upset and I'm constantly trying to keep the peace at home. \n\nBut it doesn't stop there. Then, boom, my brain is off to something else, like my church duties. I help out on Sundays, setting things up, and I'm constantly terrified I'm going to forget something important or mess up the schedule and let everyone down. And then, while I'm worrying about church, I'm suddenly right back to worrying about whether I'm messing up at work—like, did I give that last lady the right change? Is my manager watching me on the cameras thinking I'm not scanning fast enough? Am I going to get written up? \n\nIt's just this endless loop. Bouncing from the money, to the relative in our spare room, to my wife being overwhelmed, to church, to work. I just can't seem to shut it off or focus on just one thing, because the second I try to calm down about one worry, three more just pop up to take its place. It's like I'm playing whack-a-mole in my head all day long.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these worries happen almost every day, and do they feel difficult to control once they start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's pretty much every single day. I can't really think of a day lately where I haven't been dealing with it. And as for controlling it, well, that's the really frustrating part, you know? Because I try. I really do try to stop it. \n\nLike, I'll be standing there, feeling myself getting all worked up about the bills or the situation at the house with my wife and that relative, and I'll tell myself, 'Just stop. Pray on it. Leave it in God's hands, or just leave it alone for now.' I'll literally try to force myself to just focus on the customer in front of me or the bagging. But... it doesn't work. It's like, once a worry starts, it just gains momentum. It's like a snowball rolling down a hill, you know? It starts small, just one little thought about money, and then it picks up speed and rolls right into worrying about the house, and then it rolls into worrying about my job, and before I know it, I'm just standing there thinking through every single bad way the entire day could go. \n\nI'll be imagining getting fired, then coming home to my wife crying because the house is a mess and she's stressed, and then not being able to pay the electric bill... it just spirals. And I'm just standing there holding a can of soup, trying to breathe, but my mind is already ten steps ahead into total disaster mode. So, yeah, I try to shut it down, I try to pray on it, but once it gets going, it just feels impossible to actually stop the train, if that makes sense. It just keeps rolling and rolling until I'm totally exhausted by my own thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel so restless or wired that you can't sit still or need to keep moving?\n\nPatient: No, not really physically. I mean, my mind is going a million miles an hour, like I was just saying, but my body... no, I'm not pacing around or bouncing my leg or anything like that. Actually, it's kind of the opposite, to be honest. Because I'm on my feet all day at the grocery store—they give us those little black rubber anti-fatigue mats to stand on behind the register, but let me tell you, after an eight-hour shift of standing in one square foot of space, they don't do a single thing for your lower back or your heels. My feet are always killing me. \n\nBy the time I clock out and get to my car, I'm just totally wiped out. So when I get home, even with the house being as chaotic as it is right now with our relative staying there and my wife being all on edge, I just want to sink into the couch. I don't have this urge to get up and pace the hallways or anything. I mean, sometimes I'll just sit there in my recliner—it's this old brown one we got on clearance a few years ago, really comfortable—and I'll just sit there and stare at the TV. I might not even be paying attention to the show, like I could be watching a whole baseball game and not know the score, because my brain is doing that whole whack-a-mole thing with my worries. But physically? No, I'm pretty still. I'm honestly just too exhausted to be restless. So I wouldn't say I feel wired in my body, you know, just... just in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself dozing off or falling asleep during the day, even when you're trying to stay awake, like while sitting quietly or watching TV?\n\nPatient: No, actually, that's the funny thing about it. You'd think I would, right? Like I was just saying, I get home and I am just physically beat from standing on that thin little mat all day, and I sink right into that old recliner. My wife even tells me sometimes, she'll look over at me and say, \"Why don't you just close your eyes and take a nap?\" because she can see how totally drained I look. And we'll have the TV on—usually it's a baseball game, or maybe one of those home renovation shows she likes where they're always knocking down kitchen walls, just something playing in the background. And I'll be sitting there, and I might even *want* to sleep, just to get a break from everything going on in my head. But doze off? No. I never fall asleep. \n\nIt's like my body is made of lead, just totally heavy and done for the day, but my brain won't give me the green light. Because even though I'm sitting perfectly still and trying to relax, my mind is still back at the grocery store, or it's stressing about our relative in the spare room, or calculating the electric bill. I'll just be staring at the screen, completely wide awake, hearing the TV but not really processing it because my thoughts are just too loud. So, no, I never accidentally fall asleep or doze off during the day, even when I'm just sitting quietly. I honestly wish I could. A nap sounds amazing in theory, you know? But my mind just absolutely refuses to shut down enough to let me actually cross over into sleeping. I just sit there awake, feeling exhausted.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yeah, that's... that's exactly what happens. It's the nights that are really the hardest part of the whole thing. Like, I'll get into bed, right? And I'm exhausted. I mean, we just talked about how tired my feet are and how drained I am from standing on that mat all day, so physically, I am more than ready to sleep. I'll pull the covers up, my wife will turn off the lamp on her nightstand—she has this little ceramic lamp she got at a yard sale, gives off a nice soft light—and the room gets dark, and I think, 'Okay, finally, I can just rest.' 

But the second it gets quiet, it's like someone flips a switch in my brain. Suddenly, I'm not tired anymore, I'm just lying there staring at the ceiling fan. And the thoughts just start flooding in. I'll start going over the bills in my head, trying to do the math on the electric bill versus the water bill, and then I'll start thinking about my work schedule for the next week, wondering if they're going to put me on the express lane again, which I hate because people get so cranky when the line backs up. And then I'll start worrying about the house, like, is everyone okay? Is my wife still upset about our relative being here? Did I lock the front door? Did I leave the porch light on? 

It just goes on and on, this endless loop of things I need to figure out or worry about. And I'll lie there for what feels like hours, just tossing and turning, trying to get comfortable, trying to force my brain to shut up. And the worst part is, even when I finally do manage to fall asleep, it's not good sleep. You know what I mean? It's not that deep, restful sleep where you wake up feeling refreshed. I wake up the next morning when the alarm goes off, and I just feel like I never really settled down. It's like my brain was still working the whole time I was asleep, still chewing on all those problems. So I start the day already feeling behind, already feeling exhausted before I even put my uniform on. It's just a vicious cycle, really.

My head is dragging, my legs are heavy, and I'm just standing there at the register scanning items and bagging groceries, and it takes everything in me just to keep going. \n\nI actually thought maybe it was my mattress, so I tried flipping it last weekend, and then I thought maybe I was drinking too much soda in the afternoons, so I cut back on that, but no matter what I do, I just wake up feeling completely drained before the day even starts. It's just... I don't understand why a full night's sleep doesn't fix it anymore, and it makes getting through a whole shift just completely exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, even activities you once found pleasurable or interesting?\n\nPatient: Hmm, no, I wouldn't really say that. I mean, I still like doing the things I've always liked. \n\nLike, take bowling for example. I go with this group of guys from high school—me, my buddy Dave, and sometimes my cousin Mike—we go down to the lanes on Route 9 every other Thursday. I still really look forward to that. Even when I'm dragging my feet and feeling like a total zombie from standing at the grocery register all day, once I get there and get my shoes on, I'm having a great time. Because the fun is definitely still there. I still want to go to the movies, I still like grilling out when the weather's nice... like last week I made these jalapeño burgers, spent a good hour prepping the meat and getting the charcoal just right, and I really enjoyed it. So, yeah, the enjoyment hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just my battery is completely drained. But the actual liking of things? No, that's all still there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or do you wake up feeling unrefreshed even after a full night's sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, that's... that's a big part of it, actually. It's the falling asleep part that really gets me. Like I was saying before about being so tired, you'd think the second my head hits the pillow, I'd just be out cold, right? But it's almost like the opposite happens. \n\nI'll get into bed, usually around 10:30 or 11, and I'll be absolutely exhausted from standing all day, but then my brain just... turns on. It's like someone flipped a switch. I just lie there, staring at the ceiling, and my mind starts racing. I start going over the electric bill that's due next week, wondering if I picked up enough extra shifts to cover it, and then I start thinking about my schedule at the store—like, if my manager is going to put me on the closing shift again on Friday, which always messes up my weekend. And then I start worrying about my mom, you know, she lives a couple towns over and her car has been making this weird noise, and I'm lying there wondering if she's okay, if she's going to break down on the highway. \n\nIt just goes on and on like that for hours. And then, even when I finally do manage to drift off—maybe around 2 or 3 in the morning—it's not a good sleep. I toss and turn, I get tangled in the sheets. When my alarm goes off at 7, I wake up feeling like my brain never actually shut down. It's like I was just resting my eyes but my mind was still running a marathon all night. So yeah, I definitely wake up feeling completely unrefreshed, like I never really settled down at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your energy level or a lack of motivation, even when you're not physically tired?\n\nPatient: Well, hmm, it's... I don't think it's a lack of motivation, really. Like I said before, I still want to do things. I want to go bowling, I want to grill those burgers, I want to play my games. The drive to actually do stuff is there. \n\nBut the energy thing... it's tricky. Because, honestly, I'm almost always physically tired. Like, I'm on my feet for eight hours a day at the store, standing on those hard linoleum floors, reaching across the scanner, bagging heavy stuff like dog food and gallons of milk. So my body is always feeling it. I'm not really sure I have moments where I'm *not* physically tired anymore, you know? \n\nEven on my days off, like Sundays, when I don't have to go into the store and I can just sit on the couch in my sweatpants... I still feel completely drained. But I think that goes back to the sleep thing I was just talking about. Because my brain is constantly going, worrying about the bills, my schedule, my mom's car, whatever else pops into my head at 1 AM... I never really get a chance to recharge. So even when my body has been resting on the couch all day, I still feel this heavy, dragging exhaustion. It's not that I don't want to get up and do something, it's just that my battery is always at zero, even when I haven't done anything physical that day. So, yeah, I guess I'm not really sure how to separate the physical tiredness from everything else, because it just feels like it's all rolled into one big ball of exhaustion all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling down, depressed, or hopeless more often than usual, even if just for a little while?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or hopeless. It's not like that. I mean, sure, I get stressed out, like I was saying about the bills and my mom's car and work and all that. But it's not a sad feeling, you know? It's more of a... a buzzing feeling. Like my brain is just constantly humming with all these things I need to figure out or keep track of. \n\nI don't wake up in the morning feeling like there's a dark cloud over me or like there's no point in getting out of bed. I mean, I *do* have trouble getting out of bed, but that's just because I'm so exhausted from not sleeping, like I told you. It's not because I feel hopeless about the day. I actually look forward to things, like my days off, or seeing Dave and Mike at the bowling alley. \n\nI know some people get really down in the dumps, like my buddy Dave's brother went through a really rough patch a few years ago where he wouldn't even leave the house for weeks, just sat in the dark watching reruns. But I'm not like that at all. I go to work, I talk to the customers—even the ones who complain about the coupon policies, which, let me tell you, happens at least three times a shift—and I still try to make the best of things. I just feel wound up all the time, not down. So, no, I definitely wouldn't use words like depressed or hopeless to describe how I'm feeling. Just... overwhelmed, I guess. But definitely not sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you worry a lot more than you used to, or that your worries are hard to control, especially about things like work, money, or family?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That's exactly it. It's... it's just really hard to control. I mean, I've always been a guy who thinks ahead, but lately, it just feels like my mind is always jumping from one thing to the next, and I can't hit the brakes at all. \n\nLike, I'll just be standing there at the register, right? Scanning a box of cereal, and suddenly I'm stressing about money—which is always tight, but now it's just eating at me constantly. And then, before I even finish bagging the groceries, my brain jumps to my house, because we have this relative staying with us right now. It was supposed to be a short-term thing, but it's dragging out, and my wife is just so incredibly stressed about it. The house feels crowded, she's on edge all the time, and that makes *me* stressed because I hate seeing her upset and I'm constantly trying to keep the peace at home. \n\nBut it doesn't stop there. Then, boom, my brain is off to something else, like my church duties. I help out on Sundays, setting things up, and I'm constantly terrified I'm going to forget something important or mess up the schedule and let everyone down. And then, while I'm worrying about church, I'm suddenly right back to worrying about whether I'm messing up at work—like, did I give that last lady the right change? Is my manager watching me on the cameras thinking I'm not scanning fast enough? Am I going to get written up? \n\nIt's just this endless loop. Bouncing from the money, to the relative in our spare room, to my wife being overwhelmed, to church, to work. I just can't seem to shut it off or focus on just one thing, because the second I try to calm down about one worry, three more just pop up to take its place. It's like I'm playing whack-a-mole in my head all day long.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that these worries happen almost every day, and do they feel difficult to control once they start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's pretty much every single day. I can't really think of a day lately where I haven't been dealing with it. And as for controlling it, well, that's the really frustrating part, you know? Because I try. I really do try to stop it. \n\nLike, I'll be standing there, feeling myself getting all worked up about the bills or the situation at the house with my wife and that relative, and I'll tell myself, 'Just stop. Pray on it. Leave it in God's hands, or just leave it alone for now.' I'll literally try to force myself to just focus on the customer in front of me or the bagging. But... it doesn't work. It's like, once a worry starts, it just gains momentum. It's like a snowball rolling down a hill, you know? It starts small, just one little thought about money, and then it picks up speed and rolls right into worrying about the house, and then it rolls into worrying about my job, and before I know it, I'm just standing there thinking through every single bad way the entire day could go. \n\nI'll be imagining getting fired, then coming home to my wife crying because the house is a mess and she's stressed, and then not being able to pay the electric bill... it just spirals. And I'm just standing there holding a can of soup, trying to breathe, but my mind is already ten steps ahead into total disaster mode. So, yeah, I try to shut it down, I try to pray on it, but once it gets going, it just feels impossible to actually stop the train, if that makes sense. It just keeps rolling and rolling until I'm totally exhausted by my own thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel so restless or wired that you can't sit still or need to keep moving?\n\nPatient: No, not really physically. I mean, my mind is going a million miles an hour, like I was just saying, but my body... no, I'm not pacing around or bouncing my leg or anything like that. Actually, it's kind of the opposite, to be honest. Because I'm on my feet all day at the grocery store—they give us those little black rubber anti-fatigue mats to stand on behind the register, but let me tell you, after an eight-hour shift of standing in one square foot of space, they don't do a single thing for your lower back or your heels. My feet are always killing me. \n\nBy the time I clock out and get to my car, I'm just totally wiped out. So when I get home, even with the house being as chaotic as it is right now with our relative staying there and my wife being all on edge, I just want to sink into the couch. I don't have this urge to get up and pace the hallways or anything. I mean, sometimes I'll just sit there in my recliner—it's this old brown one we got on clearance a few years ago, really comfortable—and I'll just sit there and stare at the TV. I might not even be paying attention to the show, like I could be watching a whole baseball game and not know the score, because my brain is doing that whole whack-a-mole thing with my worries. But physically? No, I'm pretty still. I'm honestly just too exhausted to be restless. So I wouldn't say I feel wired in my body, you know, just... just in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself dozing off or falling asleep during the day, even when you're trying to stay awake, like while sitting quietly or watching TV?\n\nPatient: No, actually, that's the funny thing about it. You'd think I would, right? Like I was just saying, I get home and I am just physically beat from standing on that thin little mat all day, and I sink right into that old recliner. My wife even tells me sometimes, she'll look over at me and say, \"Why don't you just close your eyes and take a nap?\" because she can see how totally drained I look. And we'll have the TV on—usually it's a baseball game, or maybe one of those home renovation shows she likes where they're always knocking down kitchen walls, just something playing in the background. And I'll be sitting there, and I might even *want* to sleep, just to get a break from everything going on in my head. But doze off? No. I never fall asleep. \n\nIt's like my body is made of lead, just totally heavy and done for the day, but my brain won't give me the green light. Because even though I'm sitting perfectly still and trying to relax, my mind is still back at the grocery store, or it's stressing about our relative in the spare room, or calculating the electric bill. I'll just be staring at the screen, completely wide awake, hearing the TV but not really processing it because my thoughts are just too loud. So, no, I never accidentally fall asleep or doze off during the day, even when I'm just sitting quietly. I honestly wish I could. A nap sounds amazing in theory, you know? But my mind just absolutely refuses to shut down enough to let me actually cross over into sleeping. I just sit there awake, feeling exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that racing thoughts or worries keep you from falling asleep, making it hard to quiet your mind enough to get to sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what happens. It's the nights that are really the hardest part of the whole thing. Like, I'll get into bed, right? And I'm exhausted. I mean, we just talked about how tired my feet are and how drained I am from standing on that mat all day, so physically, I am more than ready to sleep. I'll pull the covers up, my wife will turn off the lamp on her nightstand—she has this little ceramic lamp she got at a yard sale, gives off a nice soft light—and the room gets dark, and I think, 'Okay, finally, I can just rest.' \n\nBut the second it gets quiet, it's like someone flips a switch in my brain. Suddenly, I'm not tired anymore, I'm just lying there staring at the ceiling fan. And the thoughts just start flooding in. I'll start going over the bills in my head, trying to do the math on the electric bill versus the water bill, and then I'll start thinking about my work schedule for the next week, wondering if they're going to put me on the express lane again, which I hate because people get so cranky when the line backs up. And then I'll start worrying about the house, like, is everyone okay? Is my wife still upset about our relative being here? Did I lock the front door? Did I leave the porch light on? \n\nIt just goes on and on, this endless loop of things I need to figure out or worry about. And I'll lie there for what feels like hours, just tossing and turning, trying to get comfortable, trying to force my brain to shut up. And the worst part is, even when I finally do manage to fall asleep, it's not good sleep. You know what I mean? It's not that deep, restful sleep where you wake up feeling refreshed. I wake up the next morning when the alarm goes off, and I just feel like I never really settled down. It's like my brain was still working the whole time I was asleep, still chewing on all those problems. So I start the day already feeling behind, already feeling exhausted before I even put my uniform on. It's just a vicious cycle, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, I guess it has started to bleed into my day-to-day stuff, which is really frustrating because I've always prided myself on being reliable, you know? But lately... well, take work, for example. Being a cashier, you have to be sharp, right? You're dealing with people's money, making change, scanning things quickly. And usually, I'm great at it. I've got my rhythm down. But because I'm just so tired from lying awake all night thinking about everything under the sun, and my brain is still buzzing during the day, I've caught myself making these little, stupid mistakes. Like, giving back a dime instead of a quarter, or forgetting to scan a coupon someone handed me right at the start of the transaction. It's nothing huge, I haven't shorted the drawer by fifty bucks or anything crazy, but I've had to start double-checking my cash drawer way more often than I used to. I'll literally stop and recount the change in my hand twice before I hand it to the customer, just to be absolutely sure, because I just don't trust my own head right now. And that slows down the line, which makes people impatient, and then I start worrying about *that*... it's a whole thing.

And then outside of work, it's affecting things too. I used to volunteer down at the community food pantry on Saturday mornings—just helping unpack boxes of canned goods, sorting donations, that kind of thing. It was nice, you know, feeling like I was helping out, and it's a good group of people down there. The guy who runs it, Tom, he's a retired mechanic, always has these great stories. But the last few weeks, I've just had to tell him I can't come in. I've completely cut back on volunteering because I just... I don't feel like I can keep up with everything. Between the exhaustion from not sleeping and my mind constantly spinning about my wife being stressed, the relative staying with us, the bills, the drawer at work... I just don't have the bandwidth for it anymore. I feel bad about it, I really do, because they need the help, especially this time of year. But if I go down there, I'm just going to be a zombie anyway, or I'll end up dropping a flat of soup cans because I'm too distracted worrying about whether I remembered to pay the water bill. So yeah, I've just had to step back from it, which sucks, but I just can't handle it right now.

My wife even tells me sometimes, she'll look over at me and say, \"Why don't you just close your eyes and take a nap?\" because she can see how totally drained I look. And we'll have the TV on—usually it's a baseball game, or maybe one of those home renovation shows she likes where they're always knocking down kitchen walls, just something playing in the background. And I'll be sitting there, and I might even *want* to sleep, just to get a break from everything going on in my head. But doze off? No. I never fall asleep. \n\nIt's like my body is made of lead, just totally heavy and done for the day, but my brain won't give me the green light. Because even though I'm sitting perfectly still and trying to relax, my mind is still back at the grocery store, or it's stressing about our relative in the spare room, or calculating the electric bill. I'll just be staring at the screen, completely wide awake, hearing the TV but not really processing it because my thoughts are just too loud. So, no, I never accidentally fall asleep or doze off during the day, even when I'm just sitting quietly. I honestly wish I could. A nap sounds amazing in theory, you know? But my mind just absolutely refuses to shut down enough to let me actually cross over into sleeping. I just sit there awake, feeling exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that racing thoughts or worries keep you from falling asleep, making it hard to quiet your mind enough to get to sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what happens. It's the nights that are really the hardest part of the whole thing. Like, I'll get into bed, right? And I'm exhausted. I mean, we just talked about how tired my feet are and how drained I am from standing on that mat all day, so physically, I am more than ready to sleep. I'll pull the covers up, my wife will turn off the lamp on her nightstand—she has this little ceramic lamp she got at a yard sale, gives off a nice soft light—and the room gets dark, and I think, 'Okay, finally, I can just rest.' \n\nBut the second it gets quiet, it's like someone flips a switch in my brain. Suddenly, I'm not tired anymore, I'm just lying there staring at the ceiling fan. And the thoughts just start flooding in. I'll start going over the bills in my head, trying to do the math on the electric bill versus the water bill, and then I'll start thinking about my work schedule for the next week, wondering if they're going to put me on the express lane again, which I hate because people get so cranky when the line backs up. And then I'll start worrying about the house, like, is everyone okay? Is my wife still upset about our relative being here? Did I lock the front door? Did I leave the porch light on? \n\nIt just goes on and on, this endless loop of things I need to figure out or worry about. And I'll lie there for what feels like hours, just tossing and turning, trying to get comfortable, trying to force my brain to shut up. And the worst part is, even when I finally do manage to fall asleep, it's not good sleep. You know what I mean? It's not that deep, restful sleep where you wake up feeling refreshed. I wake up the next morning when the alarm goes off, and I just feel like I never really settled down. It's like my brain was still working the whole time I was asleep, still chewing on all those problems. So I start the day already feeling behind, already feeling exhausted before I even put my uniform on. It's just a vicious cycle, really.\n\nDoctor: Has your trouble sleeping or the constant worrying started to affect your ability to function normally during the day, like at work or with your family?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess it has started to bleed into my day-to-day stuff, which is really frustrating because I've always prided myself on being reliable, you know? But lately... well, take work, for example. Being a cashier, you have to be sharp, right? You're dealing with people's money, making change, scanning things quickly. And usually, I'm great at it. I've got my rhythm down. But because I'm just so tired from lying awake all night thinking about everything under the sun, and my brain is still buzzing during the day, I've caught myself making these little, stupid mistakes. Like, giving back a dime instead of a quarter, or forgetting to scan a coupon someone handed me right at the start of the transaction. It's nothing huge, I haven't shorted the drawer by fifty bucks or anything crazy, but I've had to start double-checking my cash drawer way more often than I used to. I'll literally stop and recount the change in my hand twice before I hand it to the customer, just to be absolutely sure, because I just don't trust my own head right now. And that slows down the line, which makes people impatient, and then I start worrying about *that*... it's a whole thing.\n\nAnd then outside of work, it's affecting things too. I used to volunteer down at the community food pantry on Saturday mornings—just helping unpack boxes of canned goods, sorting donations, that kind of thing. It was nice, you know, feeling like I was helping out, and it's a good group of people down there. The guy who runs it, Tom, he's a retired mechanic, always has these great stories. But the last few weeks, I've just had to tell him I can't come in. I've completely cut back on volunteering because I just... I don't feel like I can keep up with everything. Between the exhaustion from not sleeping and my mind constantly spinning about my wife being stressed, the relative staying with us, the bills, the drawer at work... I just don't have the bandwidth for it anymore. I feel bad about it, I really do, because they need the help, especially this time of year. But if I go down there, I'm just going to be a zombie anyway, or I'll end up dropping a flat of soup cans because I'm too distracted worrying about whether I remembered to pay the water bill. So yeah, I've just had to step back from it, which sucks, but I just can't handle it right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So, no, I never accidentally fall asleep or doze off during the day, even when I'm just sitting quietly. I honestly wish I could. A nap sounds amazing in theory, you know? But my mind just absolutely refuses to shut down enough to let me actually cross over into sleeping. I just sit there awake, feeling exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find that racing thoughts or worries keep you from falling asleep, making it hard to quiet your mind enough to get to sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's... that's exactly what happens. It's the nights that are really the hardest part of the whole thing. Like, I'll get into bed, right? And I'm exhausted. I mean, we just talked about how tired my feet are and how drained I am from standing on that mat all day, so physically, I am more than ready to sleep. I'll pull the covers up, my wife will turn off the lamp on her nightstand—she has this little ceramic lamp she got at a yard sale, gives off a nice soft light—and the room gets dark, and I think, 'Okay, finally, I can just rest.' \n\nBut the second it gets quiet, it's like someone flips a switch in my brain. Suddenly, I'm not tired anymore, I'm just lying there staring at the ceiling fan. And the thoughts just start flooding in. I'll start going over the bills in my head, trying to do the math on the electric bill versus the water bill, and then I'll start thinking about my work schedule for the next week, wondering if they're going to put me on the express lane again, which I hate because people get so cranky when the line backs up. And then I'll start worrying about the house, like, is everyone okay? Is my wife still upset about our relative being here? Did I lock the front door? Did I leave the porch light on? \n\nIt just goes on and on, this endless loop of things I need to figure out or worry about. I wake up the next morning when the alarm goes off, and I just feel like I never really settled down. It's like my brain was still working the whole time I was asleep, still chewing on all those problems. I've got my rhythm down. But because I'm just so tired from lying awake all night thinking about everything under the sun, and my brain is still buzzing during the day, I've caught myself making these little, stupid mistakes. Like, giving back a dime instead of a quarter, or forgetting to scan a coupon someone handed me right at the start of the transaction. It's nothing huge, I haven't shorted the drawer by fifty bucks or anything crazy, but I've had to start double-checking my cash drawer way more often than I used to. I'll literally stop and recount the change in my hand twice before I hand it to the customer, just to be absolutely sure, because I just don't trust my own head right now. And that slows down the line, which makes people impatient, and then I start worrying about *that*... it's a whole thing.\n\nAnd then outside of work, it's affecting things too. I used to volunteer down at the community food pantry on Saturday mornings—just helping unpack boxes of canned goods, sorting donations, that kind of thing. It was nice, you know, feeling like I was helping out, and it's a good group of people down there. The guy who runs it, Tom, he's a retired mechanic, always has these great stories. But the last few weeks, I've just had to tell him I can't come in. I've completely cut back on volunteering because I just... I don't feel like I can keep up with everything. I just don't have the bandwidth for it anymore. I feel bad about it, I really do, because they need the help, especially this time of year.
